Well, I... I came in because of work. I'm a cashier, and lately at the register, I just keep getting stuck doing things that don't really fit the moment. Like, I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. 

Which, speaking of the customers waiting, there was this guy the other day buying three cans of creamed corn and he was wearing this bright yellow raincoat. It wasn't even raining out. Not a drop. It was perfectly sunny. But that yellow, it looked exactly like the life jackets we had on my uncle's boat. We used to go fishing on Lake Erie back when I was maybe ten or eleven. Caught a walleye once that was nearly as big as my arm. My uncle had this old blue Chevy truck we'd take to the marina, too... it sounded exactly like a lawnmower when it started up. He always kept peppermint candies in the glovebox.

An urge? Um... no, I wouldn't say there's an urge. I don't really feel it building up or anything. It's not like, you know, when you have to sneeze and you can feel it tingling first. A thought or a fear? Um... no, I don't think so. I don't really get any thoughts like that before it happens. It just sort of happens. 

Speaking of fears, though, I did have this terrible fear of spiders when I was younger. Arachnophobia, I think they call it. She used to serve it with this thick tomato sauce...\n\nDoctor: Before you tap or freeze, do you get a thought or fear that keeps coming back and feels hard to get rid of?\n\nPatient: A thought or a fear? Um... no, I don't think so. I don't really get any thoughts like that before it happens. It just sort of happens. \n\nSpeaking of fears, though, I did have this terrible fear of spiders when I was younger. Arachnophobia, I think they call it. My brother used to catch these huge wolf spiders in the basement and chase me around the house with them in a glass jar. Our basement was always so damp, you know? The dehumidifier we had down there sounded like a jet engine taking off. It was this old rusted thing, probably from the seventies. My dad bought it at a garage sale from a guy who collected vintage radios. He had this whole wall of them, just radios stacked up to the ceiling. I always wondered if they all worked, or if he just liked the way they looked. Some people collect the strangest things. Like thimbles. I knew a woman once who had over a thousand thimbles...\n\n[Last Question]\nJust before the tapping starts, do you feel a brief physical urge or sensation in your body that builds up until you do it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"I came in because at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t fit the moment, like tapping the scanner in a pattern or freezing with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is waiting, and last week it got me written up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A physical urge? No, doc, I really don't feel anything building up in my body before I do it. Like I've been trying to tell you, it's just that I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment when I'm at the register. I'll just start tapping the scanner in a pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing right there waiting for their change. That's why I came in, actually, because it got me written up last week. 

Which is frustrating because I've always been very careful with the money. You have to be careful with money, especially these days. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's unbelievable. I remember when you could get a dozen for under a dollar. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...

Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. 

But you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...

He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"I came in because at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t fit the moment, like tapping the scanner in a pattern or freezing with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is waiting, and last week it got me written up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, it's just this pattern I do on the scanner, over and over, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer. It doesn't serve any purpose at all. The customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and I'm just stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment. That's why I got written up last week, because it just keeps happening. 

But the scanner at work, you know, it has this red laser. Have you ever really looked at how those lasers bounce off the barcodes? It's just concentrated light, really. My neighbor has this little red laser pointer he uses to play with his cat. It's a Siamese cat, very vocal. It sits in the front window and just yowls at the birds all afternoon. Birds are so smart, though. Crows, especially. I read an article once that said crows can remember human faces. If you are mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends and they'll hold a grudge against you for years. Imagine that, a whole flock of birds just waiting for you to walk outside to get your mail...

No, doc, I really don't feel anything building up in my body before I do it. Like I've been trying to tell you, it's just that I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment when I'm at the register. I'll just start tapping the scanner in a pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing right there waiting for their change. That's why I came in, actually, because it got me written up last week. \n\nWhich is frustrating because I've always been very careful with the money. You have to be careful with money, especially these days. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's unbelievable. I remember when you could get a dozen for under a dollar. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, it's just this pattern I do on the scanner, over and over, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer. It doesn't serve any purpose at all. The customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and I'm just stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment. That's why I got written up last week, because it just keeps happening. \n\nBut the scanner at work, you know, it has this red laser. Have you ever really looked at how those lasers bounce off the barcodes? It's just concentrated light, really. My neighbor has this little red laser pointer he uses to play with his cat. It's a Siamese cat, very vocal. It sits in the front window and just yowls at the birds all afternoon. Birds are so smart, though. Crows, especially. I read an article once that said crows can remember human faces. If you are mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends and they'll hold a grudge against you for years. Imagine that, a whole flock of birds just waiting for you to walk outside to get your mail...\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever make these movements or sounds because they feel like a tic or an involuntary outburst, rather than something you choose to do?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's not a tic. I mean, it's not like a sneeze or a cough that just bursts out of me. It's just... I just do it. Like I said, I'm just stuck doing it. 

But speaking of coughs, I had this terrible cough last winter. It just wouldn't go away. I tried everything—cough drops, that terrible cherry-flavored syrup, even hot water with lemon and honey. Honey is fascinating, isn't it? The way bees make it. They have this whole complex society inside the hive, with the queen and the workers. Did you know that when a bee finds a good source of nectar, it goes back to the hive and does this little dance to tell the other bees exactly where it is? It's called a waggle dance. It's like they have their own built-in GPS system. I always wonder how they don't get lost, especially when it's windy. The wind here has been so strong lately, blowing all the leaves off the trees before they even have a chance to turn those nice autumn colors. I love autumn. The smell of burning leaves... well, you can't really burn leaves anymore, can you? The city ordinances are so strict now. You have to bag them all up in those big paper bags. They get so heavy if it rains before the garbage truck comes...

No, not really. I don't grunt or shrug or anything like that. It's really just the tapping and the freezing up that I was telling you about. 

But blinking... I mean, everyone blinks, right? You have to, to keep your eyes from drying out. I tried wearing contact lenses once, back in my twenties, but my eyes were always so dry. I had to carry around this little bottle of rewetting drops everywhere I went. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. Like I was just telling you, I just get stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment—tapping the scanner, or freezing over the open drawer with the customer just staring at me. It doesn't bring any relief, it just got me written up last week. \n\nMy manager, she actually wrote me up using a pink pen. She refuses to use blue or black ink, only pink. She says it's more cheerful for a grocery store environment. I don't know if pink is really all that cheerful, though. Flamingos are pink, and they always look a bit ridiculous standing on one leg like that. I went to the zoo in Cincinnati a few years ago and they had this huge flock of them. Or is it a herd? A flock, probably. Anyway, the smell near the bird exhibit was just awful. Reminded me of boiling cabbage. I tried to make stuffed cabbage once, the way my grandmother used to do it, but the leaves all tore apart in the water. You really have to get the temperature just right or it ruins the whole dish. She used to serve it with this thick tomato sauce...\n\nDoctor: Before you tap or freeze, do you get a thought or fear that keeps coming back and feels hard to get rid of?\n\nPatient: A thought or a fear? Um... no, I don't think so. I don't really get any thoughts like that before it happens. It just sort of happens. \n\nSpeaking of fears, though, I did have this terrible fear of spiders when I was younger. Arachnophobia, I think they call it. My brother used to catch these huge wolf spiders in the basement and chase me around the house with them in a glass jar. Our basement was always so damp, you know? The dehumidifier we had down there sounded like a jet engine taking off. It was this old rusted thing, probably from the seventies. My dad bought it at a garage sale from a guy who collected vintage radios. He had this whole wall of them, just radios stacked up to the ceiling. I always wondered if they all worked, or if he just liked the way they looked. Some people collect the strangest things. Like thimbles. I knew a woman once who had over a thousand thimbles...\n\nDoctor: Just before the tapping starts, do you feel a brief physical urge or sensation in your body that builds up until you do it?\n\nPatient: A physical urge? No, doc, I really don't feel anything building up in my body before I do it. Like I've been trying to tell you, it's just that I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment when I'm at the register. I'll just start tapping the scanner in a pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing right there waiting for their change. That's why I came in, actually, because it got me written up last week. \n\nWhich is frustrating because I've always been very careful with the money. You have to be careful with money, especially these days. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's unbelievable. I remember when you could get a dozen for under a dollar. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, it's just this pattern I do on the scanner, over and over, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer. It doesn't serve any purpose at all. The customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and I'm just stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment. That's why I got written up last week, because it just keeps happening. \n\nBut the scanner at work, you know, it has this red laser. Have you ever really looked at how those lasers bounce off the barcodes? It's just concentrated light, really. My neighbor has this little red laser pointer he uses to play with his cat. It's a Siamese cat, very vocal. It sits in the front window and just yowls at the birds all afternoon. Birds are so smart, though. Crows, especially. I read an article once that said crows can remember human faces. If you are mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends and they'll hold a grudge against you for years. Imagine that, a whole flock of birds just waiting for you to walk outside to get your mail...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make these movements or sounds because they feel like a tic or an involuntary outburst, rather than something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a tic. I mean, it's not like a sneeze or a cough that just bursts out of me. It's just... I just do it. Like I said, I'm just stuck doing it. \n\nBut speaking of coughs, I had this terrible cough last winter. It just wouldn't go away. I tried everything—cough drops, that terrible cherry-flavored syrup, even hot water with lemon and honey. Honey is fascinating, isn't it? The way bees make it. They have this whole complex society inside the hive, with the queen and the workers. Did you know that when a bee finds a good source of nectar, it goes back to the hive and does this little dance to tell the other bees exactly where it is? It's called a waggle dance. It's like they have their own built-in GPS system. I always wonder how they don't get lost, especially when it's windy. The wind here has been so strong lately, blowing all the leaves off the trees before they even have a chance to turn those nice autumn colors. I love autumn. The smell of burning leaves... well, you can't really burn leaves anymore, can you? The city ordinances are so strict now. You have to bag them all up in those big paper bags. They get so heavy if it rains before the garbage truck comes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have brief, sudden movements or sounds that you feel you have to do, like blinking, grunting, or shrugging?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't grunt or shrug or anything like that. It's really just the tapping and the freezing up that I was telling you about. \n\nBut blinking... I mean, everyone blinks, right? You have to, to keep your eyes from drying out. I tried wearing contact lenses once, back in my twenties, but my eyes were always so dry. I had to carry around this little bottle of rewetting drops everywhere I went. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. I saw a documentary where they were mourning a dead elephant, actually touching the bones with their trunks. It's amazing how much emotion they have...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't remember doing anything like this when I was a kid. It's really just been lately, at the store. 

But thinking about being a kid... I grew up in this neighborhood where all the houses looked exactly the same. They were these little ranch-style houses built in the seventies. That's why I came in, actually, because it got me written up last week. \n\nWhich is frustrating because I've always been very careful with the money. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, it's just this pattern I do on the scanner, over and over, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer. It doesn't serve any purpose at all. The customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and I'm just stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment. That's why I got written up last week, because it just keeps happening. \n\nBut the scanner at work, you know, it has this red laser. Have you ever really looked at how those lasers bounce off the barcodes? It's just concentrated light, really. My neighbor has this little red laser pointer he uses to play with his cat. It's a Siamese cat, very vocal. It sits in the front window and just yowls at the birds all afternoon. Birds are so smart, though. Crows, especially. I read an article once that said crows can remember human faces. If you are mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends and they'll hold a grudge against you for years. Imagine that, a whole flock of birds just waiting for you to walk outside to get your mail...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make these movements or sounds because they feel like a tic or an involuntary outburst, rather than something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a tic. I mean, it's not like a sneeze or a cough that just bursts out of me. It's just... I just do it. Like I said, I'm just stuck doing it. \n\nBut speaking of coughs, I had this terrible cough last winter. It just wouldn't go away. I tried everything—cough drops, that terrible cherry-flavored syrup, even hot water with lemon and honey. Honey is fascinating, isn't it? The way bees make it. They have this whole complex society inside the hive, with the queen and the workers. Did you know that when a bee finds a good source of nectar, it goes back to the hive and does this little dance to tell the other bees exactly where it is? It's called a waggle dance. It's like they have their own built-in GPS system. I always wonder how they don't get lost, especially when it's windy. The wind here has been so strong lately, blowing all the leaves off the trees before they even have a chance to turn those nice autumn colors. I love autumn. They get so heavy if it rains before the garbage truck comes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have brief, sudden movements or sounds that you feel you have to do, like blinking, grunting, or shrugging?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't grunt or shrug or anything like that. It's really just the tapping and the freezing up that I was telling you about. \n\nBut blinking... I mean, everyone blinks, right? You have to, to keep your eyes from drying out. I tried wearing contact lenses once, back in my twenties, but my eyes were always so dry. I had to carry around this little bottle of rewetting drops everywhere I went. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. I saw a documentary where they were mourning a dead elephant, actually touching the bones with their trunks. It's amazing how much emotion they have...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things like this since you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I grew up in this neighborhood where all the houses looked exactly the same. They were these little ranch-style houses built in the seventies. My dad painted our front door this bright, obnoxious yellow just so we could tell which one was ours from the street. Yellow is such a strange color, isn't it? Some people say it makes you cheerful, but I read once that if you paint a baby's room yellow, they cry more. Babies are so sensitive to their environment. My sister just had a baby, a little boy. She's been reading all these books on sleep training, trying to get him on a schedule. Schedules are so important, really. The buses in this city, though, they never run on schedule. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. I saw a documentary where they were mourning a dead elephant, actually touching the bones with their trunks. It's amazing how much emotion they have...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things like this since you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't remember doing anything like this when I was a kid. It's really just been lately, at the store. \n\nBut thinking about being a kid... I grew up in this neighborhood where all the houses looked exactly the same. They were these little ranch-style houses built in the seventies. My dad painted our front door this bright, obnoxious yellow just so we could tell which one was ours from the street. Yellow is such a strange color, isn't it? Some people say it makes you cheerful, but I read once that if you paint a baby's room yellow, they cry more. Babies are so sensitive to their environment. My sister just had a baby, a little boy. She's been reading all these books on sleep training, trying to get him on a schedule. Schedules are so important, really. The buses in this city, though, they never run on schedule. You can stand at the stop for forty minutes, and then three buses all show up at the exact same time. It makes no sense. The drivers always look so stressed out, gripping the steering wheel...\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore you freeze or tap, do you ever get an uncomfortable thought that something bad will happen if you don’t do it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. It's not a thought like that. I don't think, you know, "Oh, if I don't tap the counter, the roof will cave in." It's just... I just have to do it, and then I'm stuck. 

But talking about roofs... the roof on my apartment building has been leaking for months. The landlord keeps saying he's going to send someone out to fix it, but he never does. Landlords can be so cheap. He drives this massive, shiny SUV, one of those luxury ones that probably costs more than I make in three years at the store. I don't understand why people need cars that big in the city anyway. You can never find a parking spot big enough for them. I used to have a little sedan, a Honda, ran like a dream. I drove it all the way to the Grand Canyon once. Have you ever been? It's just... it's so vast, it makes you feel completely insignificant. The colors of the rock, the reds and oranges, especially at sunset. Sunsets are beautiful, but they always make me a little sad, you know? Like the day is over and you can't get it back. Time just keeps moving, no matter what you do. Sometimes I look at the clock during my shift and I swear the second hand is actually moving backward...

The dehumidifier we had down there sounded like a jet engine taking off. It was this old rusted thing, probably from the seventies. You have to be careful with money, especially these days. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's unbelievable. I remember when you could get a dozen for under a dollar. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. No, I don't have any disturbing pictures or thoughts stuck in my head. It's not like that at all. 

But images... you know, we actually just got these new security cameras installed at the store. Right over my register. Sometimes I look at the clock during my shift and I swear the second hand is actually moving backward...\n\nDoctor: Before the tapping or freezing happens, do you ever get a disturbing thought or image that keeps coming back and feels hard to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any disturbing pictures or thoughts stuck in my head. The smell of the chemicals—the developer and the stop bath—it was so sharp. It used to stick to your clothes, and it would give me a headache if I stayed in there too long. Headaches are the worst, especially the ones right behind your eyes. I get them sometimes when the fluorescent lights at the store flicker. They really need to change those bulbs, but maintenance is so slow. The guy who does it, Gary, he's always taking these really long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. I don't get how people still smoke, with how expensive cigarettes are now. A pack costs almost as much as a decent lunch. I usually just bring a sandwich from home, turkey and swiss, but the bread gets soggy if you put the mustard on too early in the morning...\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel a strong need for the tapping or freezing to be done in a particular exact way or until it feels 'just right'?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really feel anything like that. It's not about making it perfect or doing it an exact way until it feels right. I just do it. 

But you know, talking about doing things an exact way... people get so incredibly particular about how their groceries are bagged at the store. You wouldn't believe it. Heavy stuff on the bottom, obviously, that's just common sense, but some people want their cold items double-bagged in paper, and then put inside a plastic bag. Plastic bags are just terrible for the environment anyway. I saw this documentary late one night about the ocean, and there are these massive floating islands out there made entirely of plastic trash. It's really sad when you think about the sea turtles getting caught in all those soda rings. I've always wanted to see a sea turtle in real life, maybe go snorkeling in Hawaii or something. My uncle went to Hawaii back in the eighties. He brought back one of those little dashboard hula girls for his truck. He drove this old Ford pickup, powder blue, with a tape deck that always ate his cassettes. Cassettes were so frustrating. If the tape got pulled out, you had to sit there and carefully wind it back in with a yellow No. 2 pencil...

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF84.9, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I came in because of work. I'm a cashier, and lately at the register, I just keep getting stuck doing things that don't really fit the moment. Like, I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. \n\nWhich, speaking of the customers waiting, there was this guy the other day buying three cans of creamed corn and he was wearing this bright yellow raincoat. It wasn't even raining out. Not a drop. It was perfectly sunny. But that yellow, it looked exactly like the life jackets we had on my uncle's boat. We used to go fishing on Lake Erie back when I was maybe ten or eleven. Caught a walleye once that was nearly as big as my arm. My uncle had this old blue Chevy truck we'd take to the marina, too... it sounded exactly like a lawnmower when it started up. So, here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge or buildup right before the tapping or freezing, like you have to do it?\n\nPatient: An urge? Um... no, I wouldn't say there's an urge. I don't really feel it building up or anything. It's not like, you know, when you have to sneeze and you can feel it tingling first. She says it's more cheerful for a grocery store environment. I don't know if pink is really all that cheerful, though. She used to serve it with this thick tomato sauce...\n\nDoctor: Before you tap or freeze, do you get a thought or fear that keeps coming back and feels hard to get rid of?\n\nPatient: A thought or a fear? Um... no, I don't think so. I don't really get any thoughts like that before it happens. It just sort of happens. \n\nSpeaking of fears, though, I did have this terrible fear of spiders when I was younger. Arachnophobia, I think they call it. My brother used to catch these huge wolf spiders in the basement and chase me around the house with them in a glass jar. Our basement was always so damp, you know? The dehumidifier we had down there sounded like a jet engine taking off. It was this old rusted thing, probably from the seventies. My dad bought it at a garage sale from a guy who collected vintage radios. He had this whole wall of them, just radios stacked up to the ceiling. I always wondered if they all worked, or if he just liked the way they looked. Some people collect the strangest things. Like thimbles. I knew a woman once who had over a thousand thimbles...\n\nDoctor: Just before the tapping starts, do you feel a brief physical urge or sensation in your body that builds up until you do it?\n\nPatient: A physical urge? No, doc, I really don't feel anything building up in my body before I do it. Like I've been trying to tell you, it's just that I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment when I'm at the register. I'll just start tapping the scanner in a pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing right there waiting for their change. That's why I came in, actually, because it got me written up last week. \n\nWhich is frustrating because I've always been very careful with the money. You have to be careful with money, especially these days. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's unbelievable. I remember when you could get a dozen for under a dollar. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, it's just this pattern I do on the scanner, over and over, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer. It doesn't serve any purpose at all. The customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and I'm just stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment. That's why I got written up last week, because it just keeps happening. \n\nBut the scanner at work, you know, it has this red laser. Have you ever really looked at how those lasers bounce off the barcodes? It's just concentrated light, really. My neighbor has this little red laser pointer he uses to play with his cat. It's a Siamese cat, very vocal. It sits in the front window and just yowls at the birds all afternoon. Birds are so smart, though. Crows, especially. I read an article once that said crows can remember human faces. If you are mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends and they'll hold a grudge against you for years. Imagine that, a whole flock of birds just waiting for you to walk outside to get your mail...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make these movements or sounds because they feel like a tic or an involuntary outburst, rather than something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a tic. I mean, it's not like a sneeze or a cough that just bursts out of me. It's just... I just do it. Like I said, I'm just stuck doing it. \n\nBut speaking of coughs, I had this terrible cough last winter. It just wouldn't go away. I tried everything—cough drops, that terrible cherry-flavored syrup, even hot water with lemon and honey. Honey is fascinating, isn't it? The way bees make it. They have this whole complex society inside the hive, with the queen and the workers. Did you know that when a bee finds a good source of nectar, it goes back to the hive and does this little dance to tell the other bees exactly where it is? It's called a waggle dance. It's like they have their own built-in GPS system. I always wonder how they don't get lost, especially when it's windy. The wind here has been so strong lately, blowing all the leaves off the trees before they even have a chance to turn those nice autumn colors. I love autumn. The smell of burning leaves... well, you can't really burn leaves anymore, can you? The city ordinances are so strict now. You have to bag them all up in those big paper bags. They get so heavy if it rains before the garbage truck comes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have brief, sudden movements or sounds that you feel you have to do, like blinking, grunting, or shrugging?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't grunt or shrug or anything like that. It's really just the tapping and the freezing up that I was telling you about. \n\nBut blinking... I mean, everyone blinks, right? You have to, to keep your eyes from drying out. I tried wearing contact lenses once, back in my twenties, but my eyes were always so dry. I had to carry around this little bottle of rewetting drops everywhere I went. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. I saw a documentary where they were mourning a dead elephant, actually touching the bones with their trunks. It's amazing how much emotion they have...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things like this since you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't remember doing anything like this when I was a kid. It's really just been lately, at the store. \n\nBut thinking about being a kid... I grew up in this neighborhood where all the houses looked exactly the same. They were these little ranch-style houses built in the seventies. My dad painted our front door this bright, obnoxious yellow just so we could tell which one was ours from the street. Yellow is such a strange color, isn't it? Some people say it makes you cheerful, but I read once that if you paint a baby's room yellow, they cry more. Babies are so sensitive to their environment. My sister just had a baby, a little boy. She's been reading all these books on sleep training, trying to get him on a schedule. Schedules are so important, really. The buses in this city, though, they never run on schedule. You can stand at the stop for forty minutes, and then three buses all show up at the exact same time. It makes no sense. The drivers always look so stressed out, gripping the steering wheel...\n\nDoctor: Before you freeze or tap, do you ever get an uncomfortable thought that something bad will happen if you don’t do it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not a thought like that. I don't think, you know, \"Oh, if I don't tap the counter, the roof will cave in.\" It's just... I just have to do it, and then I'm stuck. \n\nBut talking about roofs... the roof on my apartment building has been leaking for months. The landlord keeps saying he's going to send someone out to fix it, but he never does. Landlords can be so cheap. He drives this massive, shiny SUV, one of those luxury ones that probably costs more than I make in three years at the store. I don't understand why people need cars that big in the city anyway. You can never find a parking spot big enough for them. I used to have a little sedan, a Honda, ran like a dream. I drove it all the way to the Grand Canyon once. Have you ever been? It's just... it's so vast, it makes you feel completely insignificant. The colors of the rock, the reds and oranges, especially at sunset. Sunsets are beautiful, but they always make me a little sad, you know? Like the day is over and you can't get it back. Time just keeps moving, no matter what you do. Sometimes I look at the clock during my shift and I swear the second hand is actually moving backward...\n\nDoctor: Before the tapping or freezing happens, do you ever get a disturbing thought or image that keeps coming back and feels hard to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any disturbing pictures or thoughts stuck in my head. It's not like that at all. \n\nBut images... you know, we actually just got these new security cameras installed at the store. Right over my register. They're those little black domes, and you can't even tell which way the lens is pointing. It's a little creepy, honestly, just standing there ringing up groceries knowing you're being recorded in high definition. I used to be really into cameras, actually, back when I was getting my degree. I took a black and white photography class as an elective. We spent hours and hours in the darkroom. The smell of the chemicals—the developer and the stop bath—it was so sharp. It used to stick to your clothes, and it would give me a headache if I stayed in there too long. Headaches are the worst, especially the ones right behind your eyes. I get them sometimes when the fluorescent lights at the store flicker. They really need to change those bulbs, but maintenance is so slow. The guy who does it, Gary, he's always taking these really long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. I don't get how people still smoke, with how expensive cigarettes are now. A pack costs almost as much as a decent lunch. I usually just bring a sandwich from home, turkey and swiss, but the bread gets soggy if you put the mustard on too early in the morning...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need for the tapping or freezing to be done in a particular exact way or until it feels 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anything like that. It's not about making it perfect or doing it an exact way until it feels right. I just do it. \n\nBut you know, talking about doing things an exact way... people get so incredibly particular about how their groceries are bagged at the store. You wouldn't believe it. Heavy stuff on the bottom, obviously, that's just common sense, but some people want their cold items double-bagged in paper, and then put inside a plastic bag. Plastic bags are just terrible for the environment anyway. I saw this documentary late one night about the ocean, and there are these massive floating islands out there made entirely of plastic trash. It's really sad when you think about the sea turtles getting caught in all those soda rings. I've always wanted to see a sea turtle in real life, maybe go snorkeling in Hawaii or something. My uncle went to Hawaii back in the eighties. He brought back one of those little dashboard hula girls for his truck. He drove this old Ford pickup, powder blue, with a tape deck that always ate his cassettes. Cassettes were so frustrating. If the tape got pulled out, you had to sit there and carefully wind it back in with a yellow No. 2 pencil...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The smell of burning leaves... well, you can't really burn leaves anymore, can you? The city ordinances are so strict now. You have to bag them all up in those big paper bags. They get so heavy if it rains before the garbage truck comes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have brief, sudden movements or sounds that you feel you have to do, like blinking, grunting, or shrugging?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't grunt or shrug or anything like that. It's really just the tapping and the freezing up that I was telling you about. \n\nBut blinking... I mean, everyone blinks, right? You have to, to keep your eyes from drying out. I tried wearing contact lenses once, back in my twenties, but my eyes were always so dry. I had to carry around this little bottle of rewetting drops everywhere I went. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. I saw a documentary where they were mourning a dead elephant, actually touching the bones with their trunks. It's amazing how much emotion they have...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things like this since you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't remember doing anything like this when I was a kid. It's really just been lately, at the store. \n\nBut thinking about being a kid... I grew up in this neighborhood where all the houses looked exactly the same. They were these little ranch-style houses built in the seventies. My dad painted our front door this bright, obnoxious yellow just so we could tell which one was ours from the street. Yellow is such a strange color, isn't it? Some people say it makes you cheerful, but I read once that if you paint a baby's room yellow, they cry more. Babies are so sensitive to their environment. My sister just had a baby, a little boy. She's been reading all these books on sleep training, trying to get him on a schedule. Schedules are so important, really. The buses in this city, though, they never run on schedule. You can stand at the stop for forty minutes, and then three buses all show up at the exact same time. It makes no sense. The drivers always look so stressed out, gripping the steering wheel...\n\nDoctor: Before you freeze or tap, do you ever get an uncomfortable thought that something bad will happen if you don’t do it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not a thought like that. I don't think, you know, \"Oh, if I don't tap the counter, the roof will cave in.\" It's just... I just have to do it, and then I'm stuck. \n\nBut talking about roofs... the roof on my apartment building has been leaking for months. The landlord keeps saying he's going to send someone out to fix it, but he never does. Landlords can be so cheap. He drives this massive, shiny SUV, one of those luxury ones that probably costs more than I make in three years at the store. I don't understand why people need cars that big in the city anyway. You can never find a parking spot big enough for them. I used to have a little sedan, a Honda, ran like a dream. I drove it all the way to the Grand Canyon once. Have you ever been? It's just... it's so vast, it makes you feel completely insignificant. The colors of the rock, the reds and oranges, especially at sunset. Sunsets are beautiful, but they always make me a little sad, you know? Like the day is over and you can't get it back. Time just keeps moving, no matter what you do. Sometimes I look at the clock during my shift and I swear the second hand is actually moving backward...\n\nDoctor: Before the tapping or freezing happens, do you ever get a disturbing thought or image that keeps coming back and feels hard to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any disturbing pictures or thoughts stuck in my head. It's not like that at all. \n\nBut images... you know, we actually just got these new security cameras installed at the store. Right over my register. They're those little black domes, and you can't even tell which way the lens is pointing. It's a little creepy, honestly, just standing there ringing up groceries knowing you're being recorded in high definition. I used to be really into cameras, actually, back when I was getting my degree. I took a black and white photography class as an elective. We spent hours and hours in the darkroom. The smell of the chemicals—the developer and the stop bath—it was so sharp. It used to stick to your clothes, and it would give me a headache if I stayed in there too long. Headaches are the worst, especially the ones right behind your eyes. I get them sometimes when the fluorescent lights at the store flicker. They really need to change those bulbs, but maintenance is so slow. The guy who does it, Gary, he's always taking these really long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. I don't get how people still smoke, with how expensive cigarettes are now. A pack costs almost as much as a decent lunch. I usually just bring a sandwich from home, turkey and swiss, but the bread gets soggy if you put the mustard on too early in the morning...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need for the tapping or freezing to be done in a particular exact way or until it feels 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anything like that. It's not about making it perfect or doing it an exact way until it feels right. I just do it. \n\nBut you know, talking about doing things an exact way... people get so incredibly particular about how their groceries are bagged at the store. You wouldn't believe it. Heavy stuff on the bottom, obviously, that's just common sense, but some people want their cold items double-bagged in paper, and then put inside a plastic bag. Plastic bags are just terrible for the environment anyway. I saw this documentary late one night about the ocean, and there are these massive floating islands out there made entirely of plastic trash. It's really sad when you think about the sea turtles getting caught in all those soda rings. I've always wanted to see a sea turtle in real life, maybe go snorkeling in Hawaii or something. My uncle went to Hawaii back in the eighties. He brought back one of those little dashboard hula girls for his truck. He drove this old Ford pickup, powder blue, with a tape deck that always ate his cassettes. Cassettes were so frustrating. If the tape got pulled out, you had to sit there and carefully wind it back in with a yellow No. 2 pencil...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't feel any kind of physical sensation before I do it. There's no build-up or anything that goes away afterward. It just happens. 

But sensations... you know, the other day I was standing at the register, and I got this sudden chill right down my spine. It was so weird, because the store wasn't even cold. Usually, they blast the AC in the summer to the point where I have to wear a sweater, but it was just a normal day. Sweaters are so tricky to wash, though. If you put them in the dryer, they shrink up like doll clothes. I ruined my favorite wool cardigan that way once. It was this deep forest green color, really nice. Green is actually my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind the house I grew up in. We used to build these elaborate forts out of fallen branches and old tarps we found in the garage. My brother and I would spend all day out there, pretending we were explorers in the jungle. He lives in Chicago now, works in finance. The winters there are brutal, with that wind coming off the lake. I don't know how people stand it. I prefer a milder climate, maybe somewhere down south where you can sit on the porch year-round...
