Um... no, I don't think so. I don't really get any thoughts like that before it happens. It just sort of happens. \n\nSpeaking of fears, though, I did have this terrible fear of spiders when I was younger. Arachnophobia, I think they call it. My brother used to catch these huge wolf spiders in the basement and chase me around the house with them in a glass jar. Our basement was always so damp, you know? The dehumidifier we had down there sounded like a jet engine taking off. It was this old rusted thing, probably from the seventies. My dad bought it at a garage sale from a guy who collected vintage radios. He had this whole wall of them, just radios stacked up to the ceiling. I always wondered if they all worked, or if he just liked the way they looked. Some people collect the strangest things. Like thimbles. I knew a woman once who had over a thousand thimbles...\n\nDoctor: Just before the tapping starts, do you feel a brief physical urge or sensation in your body that builds up until you do it?\n\nPatient: A physical urge? No, doc, I really don't feel anything building up in my body before I do it. Like I've been trying to tell you, it's just that I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment when I'm at the register. I'll just start tapping the scanner in a pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing right there waiting for their change. That's why I came in, actually, because it got me written up last week. \n\nWhich is frustrating because I've always been very careful with the money. You have to be careful with money, especially these days. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's unbelievable. I remember when you could get a dozen for under a dollar. My uncle used to raise chickens in his backyard when I was a kid. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. They were so loud in the mornings. He lived right next to these train tracks, so between the roosters and the freight trains blowing their horns at 5 AM, you could never get a good night's sleep at his house. The trains carried coal, mostly. You could see the black dust covering everything on his porch if the wind blew the wrong way. He had this old wooden rocking chair out there that he refused to paint...\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble understanding social cues or figuring out what other people are thinking or feeling?\n\nPatient: Social cues? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I've never really had a problem with that. I understand people just fine. \n\nBut you know, people can be really hard to read sometimes, especially when they wear those big sunglasses indoors. Why do people do that? It's like they're trying to hide from something. I saw a guy at the grocery store yesterday wearing these huge mirrored aviators while he was buying cantaloupe. Cantaloupe is tricky to pick out, you know. You have to smell the bottom of it to see if it's ripe, but you can't press too hard or it bruises. My mother used to make this fruit salad in the summer with cantaloupe and honeydew, and she'd use a melon baller to make them into perfect little spheres. She was so particular about her kitchen gadgets. She had this one drawer that was just full of things you only use once a year, like a cherry pitter and an egg slicer. The egg slicer had these tiny wire strings, almost like a miniature harp. I always wanted to learn how to play the harp, or maybe the cello. Cellos have such a sad, deep sound, don't they? Like they're mourning something...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping, is it a repetitive movement you do the same way over and over, even when it doesn’t serve any purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, it's just this pattern I do on the scanner, over and over, or I'll just freeze completely with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer. It doesn't serve any purpose at all. The customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and I'm just stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment. That's why I got written up last week, because it just keeps happening. \n\nBut the scanner at work, you know, it has this red laser. Have you ever really looked at how those lasers bounce off the barcodes? It's just concentrated light, really. My neighbor has this little red laser pointer he uses to play with his cat. It's a Siamese cat, very vocal. It sits in the front window and just yowls at the birds all afternoon. Birds are so smart, though. Crows, especially. I read an article once that said crows can remember human faces. If you are mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends and they'll hold a grudge against you for years. Imagine that, a whole flock of birds just waiting for you to walk outside to get your mail...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make these movements or sounds because they feel like a tic or an involuntary outburst, rather than something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: No, it's not a tic. I mean, it's not like a sneeze or a cough that just bursts out of me. It's just... I just do it. Like I said, I'm just stuck doing it. \n\nBut speaking of coughs, I had this terrible cough last winter. It just wouldn't go away. I tried everything—cough drops, that terrible cherry-flavored syrup, even hot water with lemon and honey. Honey is fascinating, isn't it? The way bees make it. They have this whole complex society inside the hive, with the queen and the workers. Did you know that when a bee finds a good source of nectar, it goes back to the hive and does this little dance to tell the other bees exactly where it is? It's called a waggle dance. It's like they have their own built-in GPS system. I always wonder how they don't get lost, especially when it's windy. The wind here has been so strong lately, blowing all the leaves off the trees before they even have a chance to turn those nice autumn colors. I love autumn. The smell of burning leaves... well, you can't really burn leaves anymore, can you? The city ordinances are so strict now. You have to bag them all up in those big paper bags. They get so heavy if it rains before the garbage truck comes...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have brief, sudden movements or sounds that you feel you have to do, like blinking, grunting, or shrugging?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't grunt or shrug or anything like that. It's really just the tapping and the freezing up that I was telling you about. \n\nBut blinking... I mean, everyone blinks, right? You have to, to keep your eyes from drying out. I tried wearing contact lenses once, back in my twenties, but my eyes were always so dry. I had to carry around this little bottle of rewetting drops everywhere I went. The bottle had this tiny little nozzle, and you had to be so careful not to touch it to your eye or you could get an infection. Eye infections are the worst. Pink eye, especially. My niece got pink eye at daycare last month. Daycares are just breeding grounds for germs, you know? All those toddlers sharing toys and coughing on each other. She has this favorite toy, a stuffed elephant, and it had to go through the washing machine on the sanitize cycle. Elephants are incredible animals, though. They have such strong family bonds. I saw a documentary where they were mourning a dead elephant, actually touching the bones with their trunks. It's amazing how much emotion they have...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things like this since you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't remember doing anything like this when I was a kid. It's really just been lately, at the store. \n\nBut thinking about being a kid... I grew up in this neighborhood where all the houses looked exactly the same. They were these little ranch-style houses built in the seventies. My dad painted our front door this bright, obnoxious yellow just so we could tell which one was ours from the street. Yellow is such a strange color, isn't it? Some people say it makes you cheerful, but I read once that if you paint a baby's room yellow, they cry more. Babies are so sensitive to their environment. My sister just had a baby, a little boy. She's been reading all these books on sleep training, trying to get him on a schedule. Schedules are so important, really. The buses in this city, though, they never run on schedule. You can stand at the stop for forty minutes, and then three buses all show up at the exact same time. It makes no sense. The drivers always look so stressed out, gripping the steering wheel...\n\nDoctor: Before you freeze or tap, do you ever get an uncomfortable thought that something bad will happen if you don’t do it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not a thought like that. I don't think, you know, \"Oh, if I don't tap the counter, the roof will cave in.\" It's just... I just have to do it, and then I'm stuck. \n\nBut talking about roofs... the roof on my apartment building has been leaking for months. The landlord keeps saying he's going to send someone out to fix it, but he never does. Landlords can be so cheap. He drives this massive, shiny SUV, one of those luxury ones that probably costs more than I make in three years at the store. I don't understand why people need cars that big in the city anyway. You can never find a parking spot big enough for them. I used to have a little sedan, a Honda, ran like a dream. I drove it all the way to the Grand Canyon once. Have you ever been? It's just... it's so vast, it makes you feel completely insignificant. The colors of the rock, the reds and oranges, especially at sunset. Sunsets are beautiful, but they always make me a little sad, you know? Like the day is over and you can't get it back. Time just keeps moving, no matter what you do. Sometimes I look at the clock during my shift and I swear the second hand is actually moving backward...\n\nDoctor: Before the tapping or freezing happens, do you ever get a disturbing thought or image that keeps coming back and feels hard to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any disturbing pictures or thoughts stuck in my head. It's not like that at all. \n\nBut images... you know, we actually just got these new security cameras installed at the store. Right over my register. They're those little black domes, and you can't even tell which way the lens is pointing. It's a little creepy, honestly, just standing there ringing up groceries knowing you're being recorded in high definition. I used to be really into cameras, actually, back when I was getting my degree. I took a black and white photography class as an elective. We spent hours and hours in the darkroom. The smell of the chemicals—the developer and the stop bath—it was so sharp. It used to stick to your clothes, and it would give me a headache if I stayed in there too long. Headaches are the worst, especially the ones right behind your eyes. I get them sometimes when the fluorescent lights at the store flicker. They really need to change those bulbs, but maintenance is so slow. The guy who does it, Gary, he's always taking these really long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. I don't get how people still smoke, with how expensive cigarettes are now. A pack costs almost as much as a decent lunch. I usually just bring a sandwich from home, turkey and swiss, but the bread gets soggy if you put the mustard on too early in the morning...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need for the tapping or freezing to be done in a particular exact way or until it feels 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anything like that. It's not about making it perfect or doing it an exact way until it feels right. I just do it. \n\nBut you know, talking about doing things an exact way... people get so incredibly particular about how their groceries are bagged at the store. You wouldn't believe it. Heavy stuff on the bottom, obviously, that's just common sense, but some people want their cold items double-bagged in paper, and then put inside a plastic bag. Plastic bags are just terrible for the environment anyway. I saw this documentary late one night about the ocean, and there are these massive floating islands out there made entirely of plastic trash. It's really sad when you think about the sea turtles getting caught in all those soda rings. I've always wanted to see a sea turtle in real life, maybe go snorkeling in Hawaii or something. My uncle went to Hawaii back in the eighties. He brought back one of those little dashboard hula girls for his truck. He drove this old Ford pickup, powder blue, with a tape deck that always ate his cassettes. Cassettes were so frustrating. If the tape got pulled out, you had to sit there and carefully wind it back in with a yellow No. 2 pencil...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a brief physical sensation right before the tapping or freezing that goes away when you do it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of physical sensation before I do it. There's no build-up or anything that goes away afterward. It just happens. \n\nBut sensations... you know, the other day I was standing at the register, and I got this sudden chill right down my spine. It was so weird, because the store wasn't even cold. Usually, they blast the AC in the summer to the point where I have to wear a sweater, but it was just a normal day. Sweaters are so tricky to wash, though. If you put them in the dryer, they shrink up like doll clothes. I ruined my favorite wool cardigan that way once. It was this deep forest green color, really nice. Green is actually my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind the house I grew up in. We used to build these elaborate forts out of fallen branches and old tarps we found in the garage. My brother and I would spend all day out there, pretending we were explorers in the jungle. He lives in Chicago now, works in finance. The winters there are brutal, with that wind coming off the lake. I don't know how people stand it. I prefer a milder climate, maybe somewhere down south where you can sit on the porch year-round...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted, intrusive thoughts or images that keep popping into your mind even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get anything like that. My mind is pretty clear most of the time, no unwanted pictures or thoughts just popping in. \n\nBut talking about things popping in... it's funny you mention that, because yesterday at the store, this little bird just popped right through the automatic doors. Flew right inside. It was just a little sparrow, I think, but it got up into the rafters near the ceiling fans. Those fans are huge, they look like helicopter blades, and they're always coated in this thick layer of gray dust. You'd think they would clean them, but nobody ever gets up that high. I used to hate dusting when I was a kid. My mom made me dust the living room every Saturday morning before I could watch cartoons. We had this big wooden entertainment center that held our TV—one of those heavy tube TVs that took two people to lift. Cartoons were so much better back then, you know? Like the ones where they paint a tunnel on a brick wall and someone actually runs through it. Now everything is all computer animated. It looks too perfect, too smooth. I tried watching a new movie with my nephew a few weeks ago, but I just couldn't get into it. He loves going to the theater, though, especially for the popcorn. Movie theater popcorn is the best, with all that fake yellow butter. I tried making it at home once with a hot air popper, but it just tasted like packing peanuts...\n\nDoctor: Before you tap or freeze, do you ever get an uncomfortable physical urge or sensation that goes away when you do it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't get any kind of urge or uncomfortable feeling like that. It's not like an itch I have to scratch or anything. I just... do it, and that's it. \n\nBut speaking of uncomfortable feelings, standing behind that register for eight hours a day really does a number on your lower back. They give us these little anti-fatigue mats, but they're so worn out they're basically just flat pieces of rubber at this point. Rubber is such a weird material when you think about it. It comes from trees, right? Rubber trees. I always thought it would be fascinating to visit a rubber plantation somewhere tropical, maybe Brazil or Southeast Asia. I took a geography class in college, back when I was working on my bachelor's. The professor was this incredibly old guy who used to show us slides on a real slide projector. You know, the kind that makes that loud 'clack-clack' noise every time you change the picture? I haven't seen one of those in decades. Everyone just uses PowerPoint now. It's so sterile. There was something magical about sitting in the dark, listening to the hum of the projector fan, seeing dust motes dancing in the beam of light. Dust is everywhere, isn't it? No matter how much you clean. I was wiping down the conveyor belt at work yesterday, trying to get rid of this sticky spot where someone's apple juice leaked, and I swear, five minutes later, there was already a new layer of dust settling right on top of it...\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner, do you choose to do it on purpose, or does it happen without you deciding to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I decide to do it, like, \"Oh, I'm going to tap now.\" It's more like I just... I came in because I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment, you know? Like tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while a customer is standing there waiting for their change. It actually got me written up last week by my manager. \n\nManagers are always so focused on metrics, though, aren't they? \"Items per minute,\" \"customer satisfaction scores.\" It's all just numbers on a spreadsheet to them. I remember when I first started working retail, back before everything was computerized. We actually had to punch the prices in manually on these big, clunky registers with actual buttons that clicked. You had to memorize the prices of the most common items, like milk and bread. Bread is getting so expensive lately. I went to buy a loaf of sourdough the other day, and it was almost six dollars. Six dollars! For flour and water. I tried making my own sourdough starter during the pandemic, like everyone else did. I named him \"Doughy.\" But I forgot to feed him for a week, and he just turned into this sad, gray sludge in a mason jar. Mason jars are incredibly useful, though. My grandmother used to can her own peaches in them every summer. She had this huge peach tree in her backyard, and the fruit was always so sweet and juicy. You can't get peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're hard as rocks so they don't bruise on the trucks, and they just never ripen right...\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have ongoing difficulty making friends or understanding other people’s thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble with that. I had friends, and we understood each other fine. We mostly just hung out, riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on. \n\nStreetlights were like the universal clock back then, you know? You didn't need a watch or a phone to know it was time for dinner. Kids today are just glued to their phones. I see them at the store all the time, they don't even look up when I hand them their receipt. Receipts are mostly digital now anyway, which I guess makes sense. People just want it emailed or texted to them. It's probably better for the environment, honestly. Do you know how much paper a grocery store goes through in a single day? It's absolute mountains of it. I read an article once that said a single pine tree only makes about ten thousand sheets of paper, which sounds like a lot until you think about how many receipts print out every hour. Pine trees smell so good, though, especially around the holidays. My dad used to drive us out to this farm out in the country to cut down our own tree every December. The sap would get all over your hands and it was completely impossible to wash off with just soap. You had to use rubbing alcohol, or sometimes my mom would make us use mayonnaise. Mayonnaise is such a strange condiment if you really think about it. It's basically just raw eggs and oil whipped together, but if you leave it out in the sun at a summer picnic, it goes bad so fast...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah, it really does feel like that, like my body just takes over on its own. Like I was telling you a minute ago, I just get stuck doing these things that completely do not fit the situation. I was wiping down the conveyor belt at work yesterday, trying to get rid of this sticky spot where someone's apple juice leaked, and I swear, five minutes later, there was already a new layer of dust settling right on top of it...\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner, do you choose to do it on purpose, or does it happen without you deciding to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I decide to do it, like, \"Oh, I'm going to tap now.\" It's more like I just... I came in because I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment, you know? Like tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while a customer is standing there waiting for their change. It actually got me written up last week by my manager. \n\nManagers are always so focused on metrics, though, aren't they? \"Items per minute,\" \"customer satisfaction scores.\" It's all just numbers on a spreadsheet to them. I remember when I first started working retail, back before everything was computerized. We actually had to punch the prices in manually on these big, clunky registers with actual buttons that clicked. You had to memorize the prices of the most common items, like milk and bread. Bread is getting so expensive lately. I went to buy a loaf of sourdough the other day, and it was almost six dollars. Six dollars! For flour and water. I tried making my own sourdough starter during the pandemic, like everyone else did. I named him \"Doughy.\" But I forgot to feed him for a week, and he just turned into this sad, gray sludge in a mason jar. Mason jars are incredibly useful, though. My grandmother used to can her own peaches in them every summer. She had this huge peach tree in her backyard, and the fruit was always so sweet and juicy. You can't get peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're hard as rocks so they don't bruise on the trucks, and they just never ripen right...\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have ongoing difficulty making friends or understanding other people’s thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble with that. You didn't need a watch or a phone to know it was time for dinner. Kids today are just glued to their phones. I see them at the store all the time, they don't even look up when I hand them their receipt. Receipts are mostly digital now anyway, which I guess makes sense. People just want it emailed or texted to them. It's probably better for the environment, honestly. Do you know how much paper a grocery store goes through in a single day? It's absolute mountains of it. I read an article once that said a single pine tree only makes about ten thousand sheets of paper, which sounds like a lot until you think about how many receipts print out every hour. Pine trees smell so good, though, especially around the holidays. My dad used to drive us out to this farm out in the country to cut down our own tree every December. The sap would get all over your hands and it was completely impossible to wash off with just soap. You had to use rubbing alcohol, or sometimes my mom would make us use mayonnaise. Mayonnaise is such a strange condiment if you really think about it. It's basically just raw eggs and oil whipped together, but if you leave it out in the sun at a summer picnic, it goes bad so fast...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping or freezing, does it feel completely involuntary, like something your body just does on its own?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does feel like that, like my body just takes over on its own. Like I was telling you a minute ago, I just get stuck doing these things that completely do not fit the situation. The scanner tapping, or when my hand just completely freezes over the open cash drawer, it just happens. I am not doing it on purpose, but the customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and then I get that write-up from my manager. \n\nManagers have absolutely zero patience for anything that slows down the checkout line. Lines at the grocery store are getting worse anyway because they keep replacing all of us with those self-checkout machines. You have to scan your own groceries, bag them yourself, and half the time the machine yells at you about an unexpected item in the bagging area. It is a very aggressive robot voice, too. They should really hire someone with a soothing voice to record those prompts, maybe someone with a British accent. British accents always sound so polite, even when they are telling you that you did something wrong. I tried to watch one of those British baking shows on television the other day to relax, but all the pastries just made me incredibly hungry. Baking is practically a science, though. You mess up the baking powder by half a teaspoon and the whole thing collapses into a dense little hockey puck. I actually played street hockey back in middle school, but I was always the goalie because I was way too slow to run up and down the pavement...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I wouldn't say I feel restless... it's not like I need to move around or anything like that. I actually just stand in one place for most of my shift anyway. Marshmallows are mostly just sugar and air, aren't they? We used to roast them over a campfire when I was in the Boy Scouts. Burnt food always just tastes like charcoal. My neighbor grills out every single weekend in the summer, and the smoke blows right into my apartment windows. I have to keep them shut, which makes it incredibly stuffy in there because my air conditioner is from the 1990s and it rattles so loud I can't even hear the television when it's running. The landlord keeps saying he's going to send someone out to fix it, but he never does. Landlords can be so cheap. He drives this massive, shiny SUV, one of those luxury ones that probably costs more than I make in three years at the store. I don't understand why people need cars that big in the city anyway. You can never find a parking spot big enough for them. I used to have a little sedan, a Honda, ran like a dream. I drove it all the way to the Grand Canyon once. Have you ever been? It's just... it's so vast, it makes you feel completely insignificant. The colors of the rock, the reds and oranges, especially at sunset. Sunsets are beautiful, but they always make me a little sad, you know? Like the day is over and you can't get it back. Time just keeps moving, no matter what you do. Sometimes I look at the clock during my shift and I swear the second hand is actually moving backward...\n\nDoctor: Before the tapping or freezing happens, do you ever get a disturbing thought or image that keeps coming back and feels hard to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any disturbing pictures or thoughts stuck in my head. It's not like that at all. \n\nBut images... you know, we actually just got these new security cameras installed at the store. Right over my register. They're those little black domes, and you can't even tell which way the lens is pointing. It's a little creepy, honestly, just standing there ringing up groceries knowing you're being recorded in high definition. I used to be really into cameras, actually, back when I was getting my degree. I took a black and white photography class as an elective. We spent hours and hours in the darkroom. The smell of the chemicals—the developer and the stop bath—it was so sharp. It used to stick to your clothes, and it would give me a headache if I stayed in there too long. Headaches are the worst, especially the ones right behind your eyes. I get them sometimes when the fluorescent lights at the store flicker. They really need to change those bulbs, but maintenance is so slow. The guy who does it, Gary, he's always taking these really long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. I don't get how people still smoke, with how expensive cigarettes are now. A pack costs almost as much as a decent lunch. I usually just bring a sandwich from home, turkey and swiss, but the bread gets soggy if you put the mustard on too early in the morning...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need for the tapping or freezing to be done in a particular exact way or until it feels 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anything like that. It's not about making it perfect or doing it an exact way until it feels right. I just do it. \n\nBut you know, talking about doing things an exact way... people get so incredibly particular about how their groceries are bagged at the store. You wouldn't believe it. Heavy stuff on the bottom, obviously, that's just common sense, but some people want their cold items double-bagged in paper, and then put inside a plastic bag. Plastic bags are just terrible for the environment anyway. I saw this documentary late one night about the ocean, and there are these massive floating islands out there made entirely of plastic trash. It's really sad when you think about the sea turtles getting caught in all those soda rings. I've always wanted to see a sea turtle in real life, maybe go snorkeling in Hawaii or something. My uncle went to Hawaii back in the eighties. He brought back one of those little dashboard hula girls for his truck. He drove this old Ford pickup, powder blue, with a tape deck that always ate his cassettes. Cassettes were so frustrating. If the tape got pulled out, you had to sit there and carefully wind it back in with a yellow No. 2 pencil...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a brief physical sensation right before the tapping or freezing that goes away when you do it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of physical sensation before I do it. There's no build-up or anything that goes away afterward. It just happens. \n\nBut sensations... you know, the other day I was standing at the register, and I got this sudden chill right down my spine. It was so weird, because the store wasn't even cold. Usually, they blast the AC in the summer to the point where I have to wear a sweater, but it was just a normal day. Sweaters are so tricky to wash, though. If you put them in the dryer, they shrink up like doll clothes. I ruined my favorite wool cardigan that way once. It was this deep forest green color, really nice. Green is actually my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind the house I grew up in. We used to build these elaborate forts out of fallen branches and old tarps we found in the garage. My brother and I would spend all day out there, pretending we were explorers in the jungle. He lives in Chicago now, works in finance. The winters there are brutal, with that wind coming off the lake. I don't know how people stand it. I prefer a milder climate, maybe somewhere down south where you can sit on the porch year-round...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted, intrusive thoughts or images that keep popping into your mind even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get anything like that. My mind is pretty clear most of the time, no unwanted pictures or thoughts just popping in. \n\nBut talking about things popping in... it's funny you mention that, because yesterday at the store, this little bird just popped right through the automatic doors. Flew right inside. It was just a little sparrow, I think, but it got up into the rafters near the ceiling fans. Those fans are huge, they look like helicopter blades, and they're always coated in this thick layer of gray dust. You'd think they would clean them, but nobody ever gets up that high. I used to hate dusting when I was a kid. My mom made me dust the living room every Saturday morning before I could watch cartoons. We had this big wooden entertainment center that held our TV—one of those heavy tube TVs that took two people to lift. Cartoons were so much better back then, you know? Like the ones where they paint a tunnel on a brick wall and someone actually runs through it. Now everything is all computer animated. It looks too perfect, too smooth. Movie theater popcorn is the best, with all that fake yellow butter. I tried making it at home once with a hot air popper, but it just tasted like packing peanuts...\n\nDoctor: Before you tap or freeze, do you ever get an uncomfortable physical urge or sensation that goes away when you do it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't get any kind of urge or uncomfortable feeling like that. I took a geography class in college, back when I was working on my bachelor's. The professor was this incredibly old guy who used to show us slides on a real slide projector. You know, the kind that makes that loud 'clack-clack' noise every time you change the picture? I haven't seen one of those in decades. Everyone just uses PowerPoint now. It's so sterile. No matter how much you clean. I came in because I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment, you know? Like tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while a customer is standing there waiting for their change. It actually got me written up last week by my manager. \n\nManagers are always so focused on metrics, though, aren't they? \"Items per minute,\" \"customer satisfaction scores.\" It's all just numbers on a spreadsheet to them. I remember when I first started working retail, back before everything was computerized. We actually had to punch the prices in manually on these big, clunky registers with actual buttons that clicked. You had to memorize the prices of the most common items, like milk and bread. Bread is getting so expensive lately. I went to buy a loaf of sourdough the other day, and it was almost six dollars. Six dollars! For flour and water. I tried making my own sourdough starter during the pandemic, like everyone else did. I named him \"Doughy.\" But I forgot to feed him for a week, and he just turned into this sad, gray sludge in a mason jar. Mason jars are incredibly useful, though. My grandmother used to can her own peaches in them every summer. She had this huge peach tree in her backyard, and the fruit was always so sweet and juicy. You can't get peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're hard as rocks so they don't bruise on the trucks, and they just never ripen right...\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have ongoing difficulty making friends or understanding other people’s thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble with that. Pine trees smell so good, though, especially around the holidays. My dad used to drive us out to this farm out in the country to cut down our own tree every December. My uncle went to Hawaii back in the eighties. He brought back one of those little dashboard hula girls for his truck. He drove this old Ford pickup, powder blue, with a tape deck that always ate his cassettes. Cassettes were so frustrating. If the tape got pulled out, you had to sit there and carefully wind it back in with a yellow No. 2 pencil...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a brief physical sensation right before the tapping or freezing that goes away when you do it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of physical sensation before I do it. There's no build-up or anything that goes away afterward. It just happens. \n\nBut sensations... you know, the other day I was standing at the register, and I got this sudden chill right down my spine. It was so weird, because the store wasn't even cold. Usually, they blast the AC in the summer to the point where I have to wear a sweater, but it was just a normal day. Sweaters are so tricky to wash, though. If you put them in the dryer, they shrink up like doll clothes. I ruined my favorite wool cardigan that way once. It was this deep forest green color, really nice. Green is actually my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind the house I grew up in. We used to build these elaborate forts out of fallen branches and old tarps we found in the garage. My brother and I would spend all day out there, pretending we were explorers in the jungle. He lives in Chicago now, works in finance. The winters there are brutal, with that wind coming off the lake. I don't know how people stand it. I prefer a milder climate, maybe somewhere down south where you can sit on the porch year-round...\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted, intrusive thoughts or images that keep popping into your mind even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get anything like that. My mind is pretty clear most of the time, no unwanted pictures or thoughts just popping in. \n\nBut talking about things popping in... it's funny you mention that, because yesterday at the store, this little bird just popped right through the automatic doors. Flew right inside. It was just a little sparrow, I think, but it got up into the rafters near the ceiling fans. Those fans are huge, they look like helicopter blades, and they're always coated in this thick layer of gray dust. You'd think they would clean them, but nobody ever gets up that high. I used to hate dusting when I was a kid. My mom made me dust the living room every Saturday morning before I could watch cartoons. We had this big wooden entertainment center that held our TV—one of those heavy tube TVs that took two people to lift. Cartoons were so much better back then, you know? Like the ones where they paint a tunnel on a brick wall and someone actually runs through it. Now everything is all computer animated. It looks too perfect, too smooth. I tried watching a new movie with my nephew a few weeks ago, but I just couldn't get into it. He loves going to the theater, though, especially for the popcorn. Movie theater popcorn is the best, with all that fake yellow butter. I tried making it at home once with a hot air popper, but it just tasted like packing peanuts...\n\nDoctor: Before you tap or freeze, do you ever get an uncomfortable physical urge or sensation that goes away when you do it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't get any kind of urge or uncomfortable feeling like that. It's not like an itch I have to scratch or anything. I just... do it, and that's it. \n\nBut speaking of uncomfortable feelings, standing behind that register for eight hours a day really does a number on your lower back. They give us these little anti-fatigue mats, but they're so worn out they're basically just flat pieces of rubber at this point. Rubber is such a weird material when you think about it. It comes from trees, right? Rubber trees. I always thought it would be fascinating to visit a rubber plantation somewhere tropical, maybe Brazil or Southeast Asia. I took a geography class in college, back when I was working on my bachelor's. The professor was this incredibly old guy who used to show us slides on a real slide projector. You know, the kind that makes that loud 'clack-clack' noise every time you change the picture? I haven't seen one of those in decades. Everyone just uses PowerPoint now. It's so sterile. There was something magical about sitting in the dark, listening to the hum of the projector fan, seeing dust motes dancing in the beam of light. Dust is everywhere, isn't it? No matter how much you clean. I was wiping down the conveyor belt at work yesterday, trying to get rid of this sticky spot where someone's apple juice leaked, and I swear, five minutes later, there was already a new layer of dust settling right on top of it...\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner, do you choose to do it on purpose, or does it happen without you deciding to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I decide to do it, like, \"Oh, I'm going to tap now.\" It's more like I just... I came in because I keep getting stuck doing these things that don't fit the moment, you know? Like tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while a customer is standing there waiting for their change. It actually got me written up last week by my manager. \n\nManagers are always so focused on metrics, though, aren't they? \"Items per minute,\" \"customer satisfaction scores.\" It's all just numbers on a spreadsheet to them. I remember when I first started working retail, back before everything was computerized. We actually had to punch the prices in manually on these big, clunky registers with actual buttons that clicked. You had to memorize the prices of the most common items, like milk and bread. Bread is getting so expensive lately. I went to buy a loaf of sourdough the other day, and it was almost six dollars. Six dollars! For flour and water. I tried making my own sourdough starter during the pandemic, like everyone else did. I named him \"Doughy.\" But I forgot to feed him for a week, and he just turned into this sad, gray sludge in a mason jar. Mason jars are incredibly useful, though. My grandmother used to can her own peaches in them every summer. She had this huge peach tree in her backyard, and the fruit was always so sweet and juicy. You can't get peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're hard as rocks so they don't bruise on the trucks, and they just never ripen right...\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have ongoing difficulty making friends or understanding other people’s thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really have trouble with that. I had friends, and we understood each other fine. We mostly just hung out, riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on. \n\nStreetlights were like the universal clock back then, you know? You didn't need a watch or a phone to know it was time for dinner. Kids today are just glued to their phones. I see them at the store all the time, they don't even look up when I hand them their receipt. Receipts are mostly digital now anyway, which I guess makes sense. People just want it emailed or texted to them. It's probably better for the environment, honestly. Do you know how much paper a grocery store goes through in a single day? It's absolute mountains of it. I read an article once that said a single pine tree only makes about ten thousand sheets of paper, which sounds like a lot until you think about how many receipts print out every hour. Pine trees smell so good, though, especially around the holidays. My dad used to drive us out to this farm out in the country to cut down our own tree every December. The sap would get all over your hands and it was completely impossible to wash off with just soap. You had to use rubbing alcohol, or sometimes my mom would make us use mayonnaise. Mayonnaise is such a strange condiment if you really think about it. It's basically just raw eggs and oil whipped together, but if you leave it out in the sun at a summer picnic, it goes bad so fast...\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping or freezing, does it feel completely involuntary, like something your body just does on its own?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does feel like that, like my body just takes over on its own. Like I was telling you a minute ago, I just get stuck doing these things that completely do not fit the situation. The scanner tapping, or when my hand just completely freezes over the open cash drawer, it just happens. I am not doing it on purpose, but the customer is just standing there waiting for their change, and then I get that write-up from my manager. \n\nManagers have absolutely zero patience for anything that slows down the checkout line. Lines at the grocery store are getting worse anyway because they keep replacing all of us with those self-checkout machines. You have to scan your own groceries, bag them yourself, and half the time the machine yells at you about an unexpected item in the bagging area. It is a very aggressive robot voice, too. They should really hire someone with a soothing voice to record those prompts, maybe someone with a British accent. British accents always sound so polite, even when they are telling you that you did something wrong. I tried to watch one of those British baking shows on television the other day to relax, but all the pastries just made me incredibly hungry. Baking is practically a science, though. You mess up the baking powder by half a teaspoon and the whole thing collapses into a dense little hockey puck. I actually played street hockey back in middle school, but I was always the goalie because I was way too slow to run up and down the pavement...\n\nDoctor: When you start tapping the scanner, does it feel hard to sit still or control your actions, as if you’re restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel restless... it's not like I need to move around or anything like that. I actually just stand in one place for most of my shift anyway. They give us those thick black anti-fatigue mats to stand on behind the register, but my feet still ache by the end of the day. You really need good shoes for retail. I bought a pair of those expensive walking shoes with the thick foam soles, the ones that look like giant marshmallows. \n\nMarshmallows are mostly just sugar and air, aren't they? We used to roast them over a campfire when I was in the Boy Scouts. If you get them too close to the flame, they catch on fire and turn completely black on the outside. Some people actually prefer them burnt like that, which I will never understand. Burnt food always just tastes like charcoal. My neighbor grills out every single weekend in the summer, and the smoke blows right into my apartment windows. I have to keep them shut, which makes it incredibly stuffy in there because my air conditioner is from the 1990s and it rattles so loud I can't even hear the television when it's running. Television shows just have the dialogue mixed so quietly now anyway, with the music blasting over everything..."
